# S03 audit: teacher self-modification and E02 R2 execution

## Scope and conclusion

The supplied records show a **shared teacher prompt-template change from M0 to M1**, followed by two matched E02 R2 student runs. They support adoption of several requested modeling actions, but **do not verify that either selected reconstruction matches the reference, or that M1 improved quality over M0**. No images were supplied to this audit. All descriptions of what a reference or render visually shows below are teacher or student claims, not independent visual findings.

For concise citations, every path beginning with `P/` expands to the exact absolute directory `/srv/lab-disks/disk2/users/xymo/Documents/Codex/2026-11-02/scene-rsi-rsi-1-3d-blender/outputs/teacher_self_evolution_human_pilot/`. JSON citations identify actual keys; JSONL event numbers are one-based physical lines. The two trajectory excerpts are truncated. The task-level `build.py` files repeat the respective selected step 002 builds; they are not additional attempts.

## What changed, and what can be inferred

M0 already requested distinction between observation and speculation, standalone hints and correction records. M1 adds explicit priorities for screen-space relationships, coupled position/orientation/occlusion, review of whether old advice was wrong versus unimplemented, evidence-linked correction states, and separation of successful execution from visual match. It also directs preservation of major objects, editable components, and fixed student/runner constraints. These are **instructions in shared Python prompt strings**, not a demonstrated change to student weights or a verified improvement in reconstruction capability. Sources: `P/teachers/M0/teacher.py` lines 5–46; `P/teachers/M1/teacher.py` lines 9–87; `P/self_modification/M0_to_M1.diff`, M0-to-M1 hunk.

M1's self-modification prompt refers to two development tasks with two rounds each, but their full records and an acceptance decision are not supplied here. The M0 and M1 E02 R2 contexts also contain different previous hints and previous student results: this is a matched scene and round, **not an isolated test of the policy change alone**. Both R2 students had fresh workspaces and no previous reconstruction artifacts; their inputs contain the corresponding standalone hints. Remote sampling seeds are null. Sources: `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/teacher_context.json` and `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/teacher_context.json`, `previous`; their respective `student_input.json`, `hint`, `fresh_workspace`, `previous_reconstruction_artifacts`, and `remote_sampling_seed`.

The supplied files do not state an H/M acceptance rule or its outcome. Therefore no H/M acceptance can be inferred; even an acceptance under such a rule would be distinct from evidence of general learned capability.

## Concern-by-concern chain

### Multi-object coverage and occlusion

- **Mention and diagnosis:** M0's teacher says the prior render contained the island, both cabinet groups, walls, floor and hardware. M1's teacher similarly says major objects and some relationships existed, including the wall band; it claims weak island-on-cabinet occlusion and incomplete background framing. These are recorded visual assessments, not independently verified here. The supplied prior code excerpts support existence of some named geometry, but are explicitly truncated and cannot establish full visual coverage. Sources: `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/guidance.json`, `observations`; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/guidance.json`, `observations`; both corresponding `teacher_context.json`, `previous.bounded_trace`.
- **Target and adoption:** Both updates retain the island and two rear cabinet groups. M1 specifically targets their occlusion, cropping and wall-band relationships. Both selected builds actually construct named island, left and right cabinet assets, with separate subparts: `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/build.py` lines 21–88; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/build.py` lines 29–108. This supports object construction, not recognizability or correct occlusion.
- **Verified result:** No supplied post-R2 visual assessment establishes whether the target coverage or occlusion was achieved.

### Position, orientation and framing

- **Mention and diagnosis:** M0 claims its preceding island was too small, distant and horizontal, with a narrow end face and excess left wall. M1 makes a similar claim for its own preceding result, adding inadequate cabinet cropping and rightward extension. These are teacher image claims. M0 treats camera, furniture size and layout as possible joint explanations; M1 explicitly declines a unique parameter diagnosis. Neither causal explanation is independently established. See both `guidance.json` files, `observations`.
- **Target:** M0 supplies approximate tabletop corner and foot screen anchors, a wider end face and cropped cabinets. M1 couples tabletop corners, visible faces, overhang, cabinet boundaries and occlusion. These updates directly address the respective diagnoses; see their `guidance.json`, `hint` and `correction_record`.
- **Adoption:** Both builds put the island's long dimension along the scene Y axis and create a near-end panel and side storage. Within R2, M0 moved its camera from `(-4.8,-7.6,3.45)` to `(-3.9,-6.2,3.2)`; M1 moved from `(-5.9,-8.2,5.0)` to `(-4.8,-6.5,3.3)` and changed lens 35 to 45. M1's student said it enlarged the island, but its top dimensions remain `(2.18,3.42,.105)` in both builds: the geometry was **not enlarged**, even though the camera changed. Sources: `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/steps/001/build.py` lines 39–64 and 175–177; `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/build.py` lines 21–39 and 105–107; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/steps/001/build.py` lines 36–39 and 144–146; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/build.py` lines 29–30 and 113–115; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/student_decision_2.json`, `reason`.
- **Verified result:** Camera and geometry changes are established, not the desired projected corners, cropping or match. Both status files say the final image was not seen by the student and give no quality verdict.

### Material and texture

- **Mention and diagnosis:** M0 claims weak grain and wear, overly wide floorboards and milky glass in its prior render; M1 claims insufficient wood contrast and grain. These are visual claims; M0's incomplete prior excerpt does support floorboard geometry of width `.566`, but does not establish the appearance or a unique material/exposure cause. Sources: both `guidance.json`, `observations`; `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/teacher_context.json`, `previous.bounded_trace`.
- **Target and adoption:** M0 requests narrower staggered boards, directional grain, wear and layered glass. Its selected code uses `.285`-wide staggered plank geometry, noise-based color variation and panes with opacity and shelves. The shared generated-coordinate mapping does not prove correct grain direction for every board or realistic wear. M1 asks for dark/light separation and light grain; its selected code uses darker cabinet colors, top seam geometry and varied floor colors, but contains no comparable procedural wood-grain shader. Its glass is colored box geometry without an explicit opacity argument. Sources: `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/build.py` lines 12–19, 51–59, 90–100; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/build.py` lines 5–21, 29–32, 70–76 and 99–103.
- **Verified result:** These are implementation differences, not verified material, transparency, reflection, wear or texture quality. The M1 update targeted texture but the selected program provides only limited evidence of its adoption.

### Disconnected parts and black gaps

M0 reports conspicuous black blocks at frame corners; M1 reports conspicuous black seams and asks for joined frames and panels. These are appearance claims, **not proof that components are physically disconnected**. Both updates target the reported artifact. The selected builds contain separately named frame and panel geometry, but the supplied evidence does not establish a systematic connectivity check or that the artifacts disappeared. Separate editability is not itself a defect. Sources: both `guidance.json`, `observations` and `hint`; `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/build.py` lines 25–39; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/build.py` lines 33–50. Result: unverified.

### Camera, lighting and rendering

Both hints preserve soft side lighting; M0 also says not to solve projection merely by increasing downward angle. M1's preceding diagnosis explicitly cautions against attributing appearance to a unique camera, material or light setting. In R2, both programs use a perspective camera and area lights; M1's second build reduces the second light's power from 420 to 250 and darkens assigned colors. Those are adopted program changes, not evidence that illumination or exposure matches the reference. Sources: both `guidance.json`, `hint` and `observations`; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/steps/001/build.py` lines 141–146; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/build.py` lines 110–115; both `camera.json`, `type` and `lens`.

## Technical outcome and limitations

Both R2 status files report two builds, selected step 2, no failure reason, `quality_verdict: null`, and `technical_success_only: true`; each says only step 1 was attached for student review. `state: not_started` coexists with `status: completed`, so these fields should not be silently harmonized. Stage records independently show selected-step build, save, reload and render completion, using Cycles CPU at 768×576 and 32 samples: `P/tasks/E02_M0_R2/steps/002/stage_events.jsonl` events 4–13; `P/tasks/E02_M1_R2/steps/002/stage_events.jsonl` events 4–13. The earlier teacher contexts also report successful previous steps, not an execution failure requiring repair. M1's teacher mentions a path-reading “-” notice while stating the result image was available; the underlying notice is not independently supplied. No broader technical-failure or experiment-wide conclusion is warranted.

**Bottom line:** the records demonstrate prompt-policy revision, two fresh guided builds, concrete partial adoption, and technical rendering success. They do not demonstrate visual correction, causal superiority of M1, generalization, or true learned reconstruction improvement.

## Audit conclusion

H04 was accepted under the recorded Selection rule, and two supplied subsequent student builds demonstrably implemented numerical projection checks. In the bunk-room example, executed output confirms the camera-aim sign and restoration; in the classroom example, it confirms a trial and a revised camera. These are **specific observed uses of prompted guidance**, not proof of a generally learned reconstruction capability or of visually accurate final scenes.

**Scope.** This is a text-only audit of the supplied extracts and complete files, not an inspection of the referenced images or unprovided experiments. The two trajectories are truncated; the final-build files and separate logs supply additional evidence, but do not make their omitted trajectory events audited. Teacher and student visual assessments below remain claims. Numerical projections establish where chosen 3D points project, not whether the points correspond correctly, are visible, or trace the rendered silhouette.

## Proposal and acceptance

The teacher reported that three development scenes ran and passed structural checks despite substantial framing errors: a bedroom window and chair, a restaurant pendant and depth-dependent anchor errors, and kitchen counter/hob displacement. The teacher also reported incorrect predictions about raising camera aim and unreliable bounds crossing the camera plane. Those scene-specific diagnoses are not independently verifiable from the supplied later classroom and bunk-room records. The general sign issue is supported by the later numerical bunk-room trial, but that does not independently validate the teacher’s earlier trajectory diagnosis. The targeted change was H-only: add a small explicit-landmark projection check, before/after camera trials, original-aspect handling, and a warning that projection is not visibility or appearance verification. It did not introduce a material, connectivity, or reusable M action. # Scene geometry addendum: D01 stage 4 monitor

## Evidence and scope

Let **P** be `/srv/lab-disks/disk2/users/xymo/Documents/Codex/2026-11-02/scene-rsi-rsi-1-3d-blender/outputs/stagewise_teaching_student_selfcheck_pilot` and **G** be `/srv/lab-disks/disk2/users/xymo/Documents/Codex/2026-11-26/trajectory-to-capability-rsi-hint-rsi/outputs/trajectory_capability_20261126_020200/evidence/scene_geometry`. These abbreviations resolve the cited paths exactly.

The new observations are from `P/tasks/D01/stages/04/technical_repair_build/scene.blend` and `P/tasks/D01/stages/04/visual_revision_build/scene.blend`, reported in `G/technical_repair_build.json` and `G/visual_revision_build.json`. The execution records report successful loads using the original Blender 4.2.10 binary, factory startup, disabled autoexec, one thread and no GPU. No original was saved, and no render, reconstruction, or learning trial occurred. This post-freeze audit does not update code-experiment libraries or students.

Both probes report `frame: 1`, `unit_system: METRIC`, `unit_scale: 1.0`, and `all_object_and_data_driver_count: 0`. Dimensions and distances below remain in source scene units: the unit setting alone does not establish true physical scale. Both report camera `SceneActView`, lens 22.5, the same `world_matrix`, and `render_resolution` 768 × 513 at 100%. The probe did not reapply render-backend overrides. For the historical renders, each checkpoint’s `actual_render_settings.json` records `engine: CYCLES`, `device: CPU`, `threads: 8`, `samples: 16`, and 768 × 513; probe settings must not be substituted for those records.

## Subgoal, feedback, and execution

`P/tasks/D01/stages/04/review.json`, finding `stage4_monitor`, targets `computer (obj_00626), especially monitor back and stand`. The teacher reported that the reference showed an outlined gray monitor with angled back and support, while the repair render did not clearly separate its silhouette from a pale area. The requested single correction was contrast and orientation, with the stand seated on the desktop, while preserving the camera and surrounding arrangement. The teacher explicitly could not distinguish washout from occlusion and had no extra view (`render_evidence`, `proposed_change`, `uncertainty`).

The repair’s `patch.py` defines named components `silver monitor back`, `monitor lower bezel`, `monitor stand upright`, and `monitor stand foot`, plus a keyboard and mouse. In the probe, the association of generic mesh names with these roles is an **inference from their ancestor paths and construction code**: `Box.059`–`Box.061`, `Ball.080`–`Ball.087`, and `Rod.103`–`Rod.114` lie under `silver monitor back`; `Box.062` is under `monitor lower bezel`; `Box.063` is under `monitor stand upright`; and `Box.064`–`Box.066`, `Ball.088`–`Ball.095`, and `Rod.115`–`Rod.126` are under `monitor stand foot`. These are components of the inferred same `computer` assembly, not separate monitor units.

The revision `patch.py` assigns descendants of the housing a material named `Monitor back - contrasting silver`, specifying base color `(0.31, 0.32, 0.33, 1)`, metallic 0.25 and roughness 0.48, then transforms housing and bezel about `(1.48, 2.22, 1.06)` by −8° Z and −7° X. It does not transform the upright or foot. The previous audit reports measured scene changes supporting adoption; the saved-state probe independently shows the corresponding material-name and bounds changes, not every aspect of execution or appearance. The earlier technical repair reduced the foot fillet radius after an initial build failure; it was technical recovery, not the visual correction.

In `P/tasks/D01/stages/04/confirmation.json`, the teacher’s `decision` is `advance_with_issue` and the finding is `partially_resolved`: the teacher reported a clearer gray panel but said the lower edge and support still blended into the desktop. `check_after` leaves desktop contact unverified; noise and pale tones limited assessment. This is a teacher observation, not an independently established visual gain, and the combined material and orientation changes cannot be assigned separate causal effects.

## Saved geometry comparison

The selected housing meshes change from `SceneActSurface_0.002` to `Monitor back - contrasting silver` in `material_names`. For example, `Box.059`’s evaluated world AABB changes from approximately `[1.0700, 2.2175, 1.0600]`–`[1.8900, 2.2225, 1.5400]` to `[1.0736, 2.1605, 1.0597]`–`[1.8945, 2.3374, 1.5367]`. `Box.062` also has changed world bounds, consistent with the coded panel transform. In contrast, the reported bounds of upright `Box.063` and foot pieces `Box.064`–`Box.066` are unchanged. The selected meshes remain `hide_render: false`; the cited box meshes retain 8 evaluated vertices and 6 polygons. These observations do not establish the position of the desktop surface or whether the foot meets it.

Every `all_selected_pair_lower_bounds` entry is an AABB bound, **not an exact mesh collision or contact test**. A positive `aabb_distance_lower_bound` establishes separation of that pair’s bounds; zero establishes neither touching nor correctness. Within the inferred assembly:

- Repair `Box.062`–`Box.063` has a positive lower bound of approximately 0.0200; revision gives zero. This change does not prove the bezel and upright touch in the revision.
- Repair `Box.059`–`Box.062` is approximately 0.0250; revision is zero. Conversely, `Box.062`–`Rod.104` remains positive, approximately 0.0250 then 0.0140. A separated pair does not prove the whole assembly disconnected: another component may connect it.
- `Box.063`–`Box.066` and `Box.063`–`Box.059` have zero bounds in both checkpoints. They are not proof of contact. `Box.059`–`Box.066` remains positively separated, approximately 0.1675 then 0.1672; a housing and foot can legitimately be apart, with the upright potentially between them.
- Other separated pieces can also have distinct positions within one assembly: `Ball.088`–`Ball.090` is approximately 0.1820 in both states, while `Ball.088`–`Box.064` is zero in both. Neither result alone establishes a defect or a sound join.

No cross-monitor pair is among these 48 supplied meshes; their names and ancestry associate them with this one inferred computer. Separation between unrelated monitors, if measured, would not itself be a defect either. The AABBs do not settle surface contact, hidden geometry, assembly quality, or visual quality.

## Closing record

**Confirmed saved-state facts:** the two successive checkpoints have the reported housing-material and panel-bound changes; the sampled stand upright and foot bounds remain unchanged. Camera, reported resolution, frame and driver count match. The teacher carried forward support readability and contact as an issue.

**Still unverified:** exact mesh contact or intersection, the stand’s contact with the desktop, hidden geometry correctness, independent visual improvement, the individual effects of material versus orientation, and causal teaching efficacy. No whole-scene quality conclusion follows.

**Exact coverage:** two existing checkpoints, each with 874 objects numerically observed, zero probe object exceptions, and 48 named/ancestor-filtered meshes with all 1,128 pair bounds supplied per checkpoint. These are successive states, not 874 independent experimental objects or a broad scene-quality evaluation.

## Limited visual audit

The comparison is limited to visible image appearance. Stage 1 intentionally omits furniture, so the absence of the desk, chairs, plants and other office contents in Images 2 and 3 is not treated as a failure.

### Directly visible 2D observations

- **Camera and framing:** Image 1 looks toward the back wall while showing a substantial foreground portion of the left wall and glazing. Image 2 centers the back wall more symmetrically, with both side walls converging toward it. Image 3 places the left glazing against the image edge and shows a broader, less centered back-wall area. Neither render matches the photograph’s framing exactly.
- **Room shell and proportions:** In Image 1, the back wall meets the right wall around the right-hand third of the image, and the visible floor recedes toward a low back-wall boundary. Image 2 shows a relatively narrow back wall bounded by conspicuous diagonal floor edges. In Image 3, the back wall occupies more of the frame, but its apparent proportions and wall junctions still differ from Image 1. The photograph also has a distinct overhead beam and recessed ceiling area; the renders simplify their visible appearance.
- **Left opening:** Image 1 shows tall, narrow glazed doors or windows set back from the foreground left wall. Image 2 shows a smaller-looking glazed area farther into the composition. In Image 3, the glazing is much larger and cropped by the left edge, unlike its presentation in Image 1.
- **Illumination and surfaces:** Image 1 has a clearly defined rectangular ceiling light, tonal variation on the walls, a warm right wall, and a darker tiled floor. Images 2 and 3 are predominantly pale and visibly grainy, with softer or less distinct surface detail. Neither render visibly reproduces the photograph’s rectangular ceiling light. These differences limit a direct appearance match.
- **Borders and projection:** Image 2 has a prominent black band across the top. Image 3 instead has a grainy dark strip above a bright horizontal edge, with scene content cropped at the left. Image 1 has no comparable dark top band. The renders also show different apparent convergence and cropping from the photograph, particularly along the floor edges and left opening.

### Possible 3D or implementation explanations—not established by the images

The framing and convergence differences could reflect camera position, orientation, focal length, or room dimensions; the images alone do not isolate which. The top bands could arise from scene geometry, camera framing, or rendering setup, but their cause is not visible. Likewise, the pale, grainy appearance could involve lighting, materials, exposure, sampling, or a combination. The images do not establish physical connectivity, hidden geometry, counts behind occlusion, any teacher diagnosis, or whether a student adopted a revision.

## Observational visual audit

**Scope.** Image 1 is the original photograph, Image 2 the stage-5 initial render, and Image 3 the final delivered render. The observations below concern visible image appearance only; they do not establish hidden geometry, physical connections, code behavior, diagnosis, or adoption.

### Directly visible 2D observations

- **Object coverage:** Both renders depict the main visible furniture groups in Image 1: a central island, a tall glazed cabinet at the rear left, and upper and lower cabinetry along the rear right. The photograph also shows a partial doorway or cabinet edge at the far left and a wall switch that are not visible in either render. Image 1’s right-hand cabinet has a substantial dark side panel; that end is outside the frame in Images 2 and 3.
- **Position and direction:** In Image 1, the island top recedes toward the rear left, and its near end is prominent. In Images 2 and 3, the island reads more horizontally across the frame, with its long cabinet face prominent. The rear cabinet groups are recognizable but appear more separated and front-facing in the renders. Image 3 frames the island larger than Image 2.
- **Visible contacts:** In both renders, the island top visually meets its base, and the rear countertop visually meets the lower cabinets. The left upper cabinet also appears to sit on its lower section. These image contacts do not prove that the modeled parts are physically connected. The upper-right cabinets are visually separated from the countertop by a light-colored opening, with a visible vertical support at its left edge; Image 1 likewise shows an open backsplash area beneath those cabinets.
- **Components and detail:** The renders include framed doors, drawer fronts, handles, and glazing-like pale panels. Compared with Image 1, the panels in Images 2 and 3 look more uniformly opaque and pale, without the photograph’s pronounced reflections and varied transparency. The rendered island face has a regular repeated arrangement; the photograph shows a different visible arrangement of doors and hardware from its camera angle.
- **Material and texture:** Image 1 has darker, varied wood grain on the cabinetry, a golden island top with conspicuous grain, and irregular color variation in the floorboards. Images 2 and 3 have lighter, more uniform surfaces; their floor and island top show fine, regular linear patterns. The rendered cabinet wood does retain visible grain and color contrast with the top.
- **Lighting:** Image 1 has uneven natural-looking highlights and darker cabinet recesses. The renders are brighter and more evenly lit, with soft shadows under and behind the island. Images 2 and 3 also show visible grainy render noise, particularly around shadowed cabinet edges.
- **Camera and borders:** The photograph fills its frame with the room and furniture. Both renders show a conspicuous black area at the left where the light wall and floor end in the image. Image 2 crops the right cabinet run and places more empty wall above the furniture; Image 3 is a closer view but still crops the right-hand run and retains the black left border.

### Possible 3D or implementation interpretations

The black region could reflect limited room surfaces, background, or camera framing; the images alone do not identify which. The change in apparent island proportions and cabinet orientation could arise from camera placement, projection, geometry, or a combination. Occluded parts and actual object connectivity cannot be checked from these views. The differences between Images 2 and 3 show a changed visible composition, but do not by themselves establish what was changed in the scene or why.

## Limited visual audit

The images show a broadly similar arrangement: a central wood-topped island, cabinetry along the rear walls, and a wood floor. The following comparisons concern visible appearance, not the correctness of hidden geometry.

### Directly visible 2D observations

- **Cabinet color and surface character:** The cabinetry in the photograph is dark, varied brown wood with visible grain and shading (Image 1). Both renders use lighter, more uniform brown cabinetry (Images 2 and 3). The island top is also paler and more even in the renders than in the photograph.
- **Upper cabinet details:** The photographed upper cabinets have comparatively substantial dark wood frames and glazing with reflections and horizontal divisions (Image 1). The rendered glazing looks more opaque and uniformly pale, while black marks at frame intersections and edges are conspicuous (Images 2 and 3). These marks are particularly prominent across the upper cabinets in both renders.
- **Rear cabinet arrangement:** In the photograph, the right-hand upper cabinet sits above an open backsplash and dark counter, with relatively few broad framed sections visible (Image 1). Image 2 depicts tall, narrow upper sections without an obvious middle horizontal division; Image 3 depicts a wider run of narrow sections with a prominent middle rail. Neither visible pattern closely matches every division in the photograph.
- **Island proportions and detailing:** The photographed island has a thick overhanging top and a dark base with recessed panels, drawers, doors and visible hardware (Image 1). Both renders show these broad features, but their island fronts have more regularly repeated, sharply outlined panels and conspicuous light or dark hardware marks (Images 2 and 3). The photographed base appears less uniformly segmented.
- **Floor and surroundings:** The photographed floor has varied narrow boards and noticeable wear or small dark marks (Image 1). The rendered floors look cleaner and more regular, with more conspicuous plank outlines (Images 2 and 3). The right side of each render also exposes a large plain wall area not presented in the same way in the photograph; the framing and viewpoint differ, so this is not a direct inventory of the room.
- **Viewpoint and composition:** The island occupies the foreground in all three images, but its apparent placement and the amount of surrounding cabinetry differ. Image 3 shows a long rear cabinet run behind the island, while Image 2 shows a shorter run and more empty wall to the right. Those differences are visible in the pictures, although perspective and framing affect the comparison.

### Geometry or implementation hypotheses—not established by the images

The dark marks at cabinet-frame junctions in Images 2 and 3 could reflect modeled hardware, intersections, materials, or rendering artifacts; the images alone do not establish which. Similarly, apparent panel borders, overhangs and cabinet alignment suggest possible differences in modeled proportions or construction, but do not prove physical connectivity or reveal geometry behind occlusion. The images provide no direct evidence of the underlying code, a teacher’s diagnosis, or what guidance either student adopted.

Images 2 and 3 are separate, history-dependent R2 generations, not a common-state intervention. Their visible differences do not establish a causal M1 effect, a universal quality ranking, or a conclusion about other experiments.
